Projects

Discussion questions

Do you write scripts? Do you organize them in folders?

Do you use version control? Do you back-up your scripts?

Where do your scripts and data live? How does the script know that?

Do you share their code with others? With whom? How?

Do others share their code with you? Who? How?

Photo by Headway on Unsplash

setwd()

  • “/” or “\\” depending on the OS!

The (absolute) path needs to be updated WHEN you:

  • move your script around

  • work from a different device, a server…

  • share your script with someone else

Project-based workflow

  • R projects, git repositories…

  • Portable: relevant code and data together

  • Paths relative to the root of a project

Project oriented workflows

{here}

library(here)

i_am("index.qmd")
# here()
here() |> dir() |> length() |> print()
[1] 6
here("analysis") |> dir() |> print()  
[1] "model.R"
source(here("analysis", "model.R"))   
[1] "These are the contents of model.R"
  1. Nothing happens as long as the path is the path of this file relative to the root of the project.
  2. Get the number of files/directories in the root of the project
  3. Get the contents of the “analysis” directory
  4. Source the “model.R” script in the “analysis” directory.

{here}

git

  • R projects can be git repositories

  • Version control: keep track of the changes in your code, data, output…

  • Share and collaborate via GitLab, Github…

Happy Git With R

{renv}

  • Can be good practice, but it’s not as necessary as with Python
  • Keeps track of R and package versions
library(renv)
init()
install.packages("tidyverse")
install.packages("reprex")
snapshot()
# Someone else uses your project
restore()
1
Initialize your virtual environment.
2
Install some packages (in the environment).
3
Register the status.
4
In another system, recover the status.

R users don’t deal with different versions of R packages like Python users do with Python modules, since they are mostly centrally managed by CRAN or other repositories. But it can still be useful practice in some case, “if you really care about reproducibility” (in Hadley Wickham’s words).

{renv}

Debugging

Discussion questions

What do you do when your code fails? Where do you search for answers?

How do you look into your code to find the problematic part?

Do you write your own functions?

{reprex}: Minimal reproducible examples

There is some code to reproduce…

sth_is_wrong.R
library(nycflights13)
df <- head(flights)
for (i in 1:length(df$dep_delay)) {
  if (is.na(df$dep_delay[[i]])) {
    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- NA
  } else if(df$dep_delay[[i]] < -30) {
    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- delay_categories[[1]]
  } else if(df$dep_delay[[i]] < 30) {
    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- delay_categories[[2]]
  } else {
    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- delay_categories[[3]]
  }
}
delay_categories <- c("Early", "Kind of on time", "Late")

{reprex}: Minimal reproducible examples

In the console (or with the RStudio add-on):

library(reprex)
reprex(here("R", "sth_is_wrong.R"))

sth_is_wrong_reprex.md is created

    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- delay_categories[[2]]

    df[i, "dep_delay_cat"] <- delay_categories[[3]]
  }

Created on 2026-10-19 with reprex v2.1.1

Interactive debugging

  • browser()
  • Breakpoints

Tips for R scripts

Tips include:

  • Use the comment style in RStudio to generate headings with hierarchies, for folding but also to have an outline and quick links to different regions

  • Do not save .Rdata or history, so you can always restart the session in a clean environment (because you don’t know what leftovers there are and how they can impact what you’re doing next).

Style

Discussion questions

Do you agree with others on naming style, spaces, punctuation…?

Are you aware of any stylesheet for R code?

If someone wants to argue your preferences, what would you say?

Do you feel that others have a different way of writing R code than you?

If so, does that affect you? Does that affect the way you can read/understand their code?

Photo by Headway on Unsplash

Naming: Best practices

  1. Beware of / avoid using existing names (e.g. df, c, T, mean)
  2. Avoid using dots (although Base R does use them)
  3. For files: stick to numbers, lowercase letters, _ and - - beware of case!
  4. For variables: use lowercase letters, numbers and snake_case.
  5. Generally: variables = nouns; functions = verbs

Tidyverse styleguide

Punctuation best practices

  • No spaces between () and text inside.
  • Use "" instead of '' unless there is already "" inside.
  • The assignment operator in R is <-. 1
  • The assignment operator and infix operators should be surrounded by spaces.
  • Spaces around the () for for, if and when.
  • No spaces around :, $, [, ^, +…
  • Spaces only after () for function arguments.
  • Difference between [] and [[]].
  • Pay attention to indentation!

Tidyverse styleguide

  1. Use Alt+- / ⌥+-!

The pipe and other data-wrangling tricks

Discussion questions

Do you know what the pipe is in R/coding, what it is used for?

Do you use the pipe?

  1. If so, the tidyverse one from {magrittr} or the native one?

  2. Do you have a preference? Which? Why?

  3. If you do know about the pipe but don’t use it, why?

Do you use tidyverse packages (tibbles…)? Why (not)?

If yes, do you use case_when() or long conditionals? (Show example of the conditional)

Photo by Headway on Unsplash

What we’ll do:

library(tidyverse)
path <- here("data", "nycflights13_random2000.csv")
  • Compare readr::read_csv() and read.csv()
  • Look at the {dplyr} functions select(), filter() and mutate() and their helpers
  • Compare case_when() with a chain of conditionals
  • Chain functions with the pipe

mutate() on one column

Important

mutate() modifies the column and returns the dataframe without overwriting it!

# readr_csv$month <- month.name(readr_csv$month); readr_csv
mutate(readr_csv, month = month.name[month])

Let’s categorize a continuous variable

delay_categories <- c("Early", "On time", "Late")
Delay \(x\) Category
NA NA
\(x <= -30\) “Early”
\(-30 < x <= 30\) “On time”
\(x < -30\) “Late”

Chain of conditionals

for (i in seq_along(base_csv$dep_delay)) {
  if (is.na(base_csv$dep_delay[[i]])) {
    base_csv[i, "dep_delay_category"] <- NA
  } else if (base_csv$dep_delay[[i]] <= -30) {
    base_csv[i, "dep_delay_category"] <- delay_categories[[1]]
  } else if (base_csv$dep_delay[[i]] <= 30) {
    base_csv[i, "dep_delay_category"] <- delay_categories[[2]]
  } else {
    base_csv[i, "dep_delay_category"] <- delay_categories[[3]]
  }
}
base_csv$dep_delay_category <- factor(base_csv$dep_delay_category, levels = delay_categories)
table(base_csv$dep_delay_category)

  Early On time    Late 
      0    1679     278

case_when()

readr_csv <- mutate(
  readr_csv,
  dep_delay_category = factor(
    case_when(
      is.na(dep_delay) ~ NA,
      dep_delay <= -30 ~ delay_categories[[1]],
      dep_delay <= 30 ~ delay_categories[[2]],
      TRUE ~ delay_categories[[3]]
    ), levels = delay_categories
  )
)
table(readr_csv$dep_delay_category)

  Early On time    Late 
      0    1679     278

  mutate(dep_delay_category = case_when(

Wrangling and assigning

dataset <- readr_csv |> 
  select(-dep_delay_category) |> # from previous slides
  filter(!is.na(dep_delay), !is.na(arr_delay)) |> 
  mutate(
    month_name = month.name[month],
    across(
      ends_with("delay"),
      \(delay) case_when(
        is.na(delay) ~ NA,
        delay <= -30 ~ delay_categories[[1]],
        delay <= 30 ~ delay_categories[[2]],
        TRUE ~ delay_categories[[3]]
      ) |> factor(levels=delay_categories),
      .names = "{.col}_cat"
      ),
    across(where(is.character), as.factor)
    )
dataset |> select(time_hour, contains("delay"))

The pipe and tidyverse: take-aways

Photo by Mika Baumeister on Unsplash

I/O

Discussion questions

What is the format of your input data?

What is the format of your output data?

Are the files plain text? Are they interoperable (can they be opened by different applications)?

How do you combine the output of your scripts into the writing you have to provide (papers, reports, presentations…)

Photo by Headway on Unsplash

  • {readr}
  • File formats / extensions

I have two more slides (hidden) about readr parsing columns and one comparing it to base R functions but it might be too much

X-separated values…

Values separated by spaces (readr::read_table())

col1 col2 col3
1.5 2.2 3
4 5 6
7 8 9

Comma separated values (readr::read_csv())

col1,col2,col3
1.5,2.2,3
4,5,6
7,8,9

Values separated by semicolons (readr::read_csv2())

col1;col2;col3
1,5;2,2;3
4;5;6
7;8;9

Tab-separated values (readr::read_tsv())

col1    col2    col3
1,5 something;semicolon 3
text,with,commas    wha a a t   6
7   8   9

.rds, .rda, .Rdata, other formats

File type Save Open Pros Cons
.rda/.Rdata1 save() load() Save multiple R objects as they are Only R can open it; modifies variables
.rds saveRDS() readRDS() Save single R object as it is Only R can open it.
.txt, .csv… write.csv()… read.csv()… Plain text: interoperable Not just any R object

  1. Outside of (data) packages.
